Yeah… I guess what finally got me to come in is I keep jumping out of my skin over stupid stuff. Like at work, if a breaker snaps or somebody drops a tool, or even if someone just walks up behind me, my whole body reacts before I even know what happened. My heart starts pounding and it just keeps going, long after everybody else is already back to normal.

It’s embarrassing, honestly. I do maintenance work, so loud noises aren’t exactly rare, you know? I used to be better with it. Now I’m the guy flinching like something serious just happened. And then I’m standing there trying to act like I’m fine, but I’m not.

It’s mostly the specific stuff, yeah… like a breaker snapping, a tool hitting the floor, somebody coming up behind me when I don’t hear them. But then once it happens, I don’t just settle down right away. My chest keeps going like I ran up stairs or something.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know… I mean, I haven’t really sat and measured it like that. Most of the times I notice it, there’s something that kicks it off, like a bang or someone coming up on me.

When it’s quiet, I guess I’m not thinking, “Oh, I’m jittery,” you know? I’m usually trying to get through whatever job’s in front of me. Half the time at work there’s always some noise anyway, so it’s hard to tell what counts as nothing happening. Like the other day I was changing out a fixture and the hallway was quiet, but then the cart wheels started squeaking from way down the hall and I still looked over like… anyway, I don’t know if that answers it.

I guess I haven’t noticed it much when there’s really nothing going on.

I’m not really sure. I mean, I’m not alone in complete silence that much, or I don’t notice it that way. I don’t know that I’d call it anxious, exactly. Like if it’s actually peaceful, I’m usually… I don’t know, I’m waiting for the next thing I need to do, I guess. I’m not great at just sitting there.

But quiet is funny, too, because quiet isn’t really quiet. There’s always something—like the vents, somebody outside with a truck backing up, the building settling. At work, even when it’s supposed to be calm, there’s always some little noise from somewhere. So I’m not sure I’ve noticed feeling anxious just because it’s peaceful. It’s more like I notice it when something cuts into that quiet, if that makes sense.

I mean… I don’t really know. Completely quiet like that doesn’t happen much for me. I mean… I’m trying to picture that, but I don’t know. Like the other day I was changing out a fixture and the hallway was quiet, but then the cart wheels started squeaking from way down the hall and I still looked over like… anyway, I don’t know if that answers it.\n\nI guess I haven’t noticed it much when there’s really nothing going on.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or on edge when you're alone and there's complete silence?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I’m not alone in complete silence that much, or I don’t notice it that way. There’s always the fridge kicking on, traffic outside, pipes making noise… old apartments make all kinds of sounds, you know? Mine’s got this heater that knocks like somebody’s messing with it, and I’ve been meaning to call the landlord, but then you know how that goes.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I feel anxious just from silence itself. I guess I haven’t paid attention to it like that. Most of what I notice is when something happens suddenly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're in a quiet, peaceful environment with no sudden noises?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it anxious, exactly. Like if it’s actually peaceful, I’m usually… I don’t know, I’m waiting for the next thing I need to do, I guess. I’m not great at just sitting there.\n\nBut quiet is funny, too, because quiet isn’t really quiet. There’s always something—like the vents, somebody outside with a truck backing up, the building settling. At work, even when it’s supposed to be calm, there’s always some little noise from somewhere. So I’m not sure I’ve noticed feeling anxious just because it’s peaceful. It’s more like I notice it when something cuts into that quiet, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you are in a completely quiet and peaceful environment with no distractions or noises?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t really know. Completely quiet like that doesn’t happen much for me. Even when it’s supposed to be quiet, I start hearing little stuff, like the refrigerator or somebody outside or the lights buzzing, and then I’m thinking about whether I left something in the truck or if I need to pick up parts after work.\n\nSo I can’t really say, “Yeah, I’m anxious in total silence,” because I don’t know if I’ve been in that exact kind of situation. I don’t usually sit still long enough to notice it that cleanly, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious if you were in a completely quiet room with no noise at all?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’m trying to picture that, but I don’t know. A completely quiet room sounds almost like one of those test rooms or something, you know, with the padded walls—my buddy used to work doing electrical in a studio, and he said the quiet in there was weird, like you could hear yourself swallowing.\n\nSo maybe I’d feel… uncomfortable? I’m not sure I’d call it anxious. I don’t think I’ve really been somewhere with no noise at all. Even when I’m home and it seems quiet, there’s always something humming or clicking. So I don’t know how I’d react until I was actually sitting in it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yeah… I mean, if I’m really not expecting anything, that’s kind of when it gets me worse. Like at work, I’ll be doing something normal—checking a panel or reaching for a tool—and if a breaker snaps or somebody drops something behind me, I just about come out of my boots.

Yeah, that’s kind of the thing… it can be going fine, totally normal, and then something small happens and I’m gone. Like a breaker snaps, or a tool hits the floor, or somebody just comes up behind me to ask a question—I jump like I’m about to get hit or something.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.0, F41.1, F41.8, F43.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess what finally got me to come in is I keep jumping out of my skin over stupid stuff. Like at work, if a breaker snaps or somebody drops a tool, or even if someone just walks up behind me, my whole body reacts before I even know what happened. My heart starts pounding and it just keeps going, long after everybody else is already back to normal.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly. I do maintenance work, so loud noises aren’t exactly rare, you know? I used to be better with it. Now I’m the guy flinching like something serious just happened. And then I’m standing there trying to act like I’m fine, but I’m not.\n\nDoctor: Are these reactions happening in response to specific noises or surprises, or are you feeling on edge and jittery even when nothing out of the ordinary is happening?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the specific stuff, yeah… like a breaker snapping, a tool hitting the floor, somebody coming up behind me when I don’t hear them. But then once it happens, I don’t just settle down right away. My chest keeps going like I ran up stairs or something.\n\nI mean, maintenance work is full of that kind of noise, which is the stupid part. There’s always something clanging around, guys yelling across the room, carts banging into doorframes… I used to just tune it out. Now one sharp sound and I’m halfway turned around before I even know what I’m doing, and everyone else is already back to work while I’m still trying to get my heart to calm down.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel on edge or jittery even when nothing out of the ordinary is happening and there are no loud noises around you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I haven’t really sat and measured it like that. Most of the times I notice it, there’s something that kicks it off, like a bang or someone coming up on me.\n\nWhen it’s quiet, I guess I’m not thinking, “Oh, I’m jittery,” you know? I’m usually trying to get through whatever job’s in front of me. Half the time at work there’s always some noise anyway, so it’s hard to tell what counts as nothing happening. Like the other day I was changing out a fixture and the hallway was quiet, but then the cart wheels started squeaking from way down the hall and I still looked over like… anyway, I don’t know if that answers it.\n\nI guess I haven’t noticed it much when there’s really nothing going on.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or on edge when you're alone and there's complete silence?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I’m not alone in complete silence that much, or I don’t notice it that way. There’s always the fridge kicking on, traffic outside, pipes making noise… old apartments make all kinds of sounds, you know? Mine’s got this heater that knocks like somebody’s messing with it, and I’ve been meaning to call the landlord, but then you know how that goes.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I feel anxious just from silence itself. I guess I haven’t paid attention to it like that. So I’m not sure I’ve noticed feeling anxious just because it’s peaceful. I don’t usually sit still long enough to notice it that cleanly, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious if you were in a completely quiet room with no noise at all?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’m trying to picture that, but I don’t know. I don’t know… when nothing’s happening, I guess I don’t really pay attention to it that way. Like if I’m just sitting there and not thinking about work, sometimes I’m just watching TV or messing with my phone, and then I’ll realize I didn’t even hear half of what was on.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess what finally got me to come in is I keep jumping out of my skin over stupid stuff. Like at work, if a breaker snaps or somebody drops a tool, or even if someone just walks up behind me, my whole body reacts before I even know what happened. My heart starts pounding and it just keeps going, long after everybody else is already back to normal.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly. I do maintenance work, so loud noises aren’t exactly rare, you know? I used to be better with it. Now I’m the guy flinching like something serious just happened. And then I’m standing there trying to act like I’m fine, but I’m not.\n\nDoctor: Are these reactions happening in response to specific noises or surprises, or are you feeling on edge and jittery even when nothing out of the ordinary is happening?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the specific stuff, yeah… like a breaker snapping, a tool hitting the floor, somebody coming up behind me when I don’t hear them. But then once it happens, I don’t just settle down right away. My chest keeps going like I ran up stairs or something.\n\nI mean, maintenance work is full of that kind of noise, which is the stupid part. There’s always something clanging around, guys yelling across the room, carts banging into doorframes… I used to just tune it out. Now one sharp sound and I’m halfway turned around before I even know what I’m doing, and everyone else is already back to work while I’m still trying to get my heart to calm down.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel on edge or jittery even when nothing out of the ordinary is happening and there are no loud noises around you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I haven’t really sat and measured it like that. Most of the times I notice it, there’s something that kicks it off, like a bang or someone coming up on me.\n\nWhen it’s quiet, I guess I’m not thinking, “Oh, I’m jittery,” you know? I’m usually trying to get through whatever job’s in front of me. Half the time at work there’s always some noise anyway, so it’s hard to tell what counts as nothing happening. Like the other day I was changing out a fixture and the hallway was quiet, but then the cart wheels started squeaking from way down the hall and I still looked over like… anyway, I don’t know if that answers it.\n\nI guess I haven’t noticed it much when there’s really nothing going on.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or on edge when you're alone and there's complete silence?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I’m not alone in complete silence that much, or I don’t notice it that way. There’s always the fridge kicking on, traffic outside, pipes making noise… old apartments make all kinds of sounds, you know? Mine’s got this heater that knocks like somebody’s messing with it, and I’ve been meaning to call the landlord, but then you know how that goes.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I feel anxious just from silence itself. I guess I haven’t paid attention to it like that. Most of what I notice is when something happens suddenly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're in a quiet, peaceful environment with no sudden noises?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it anxious, exactly. There’s always something—like the vents, somebody outside with a truck backing up, the building settling. At work, even when it’s supposed to be calm, there’s always some little noise from somewhere. So I’m not sure I’ve noticed feeling anxious just because it’s peaceful. It’s more like I notice it when something cuts into that quiet, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you are in a completely quiet and peaceful environment with no distractions or noises?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t really know. Completely quiet like that doesn’t happen much for me. Even when it’s supposed to be quiet, I start hearing little stuff, like the refrigerator or somebody outside or the lights buzzing, and then I’m thinking about whether I left something in the truck or if I need to pick up parts after work.\n\nSo I can’t really say, “Yeah, I’m anxious in total silence,” because I don’t know if I’ve been in that exact kind of situation. I don’t usually sit still long enough to notice it that cleanly, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious if you were in a completely quiet room with no noise at all?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’m trying to picture that, but I don’t know. I mean… relaxing is kind of a funny word, I guess. Like, I can sit down with the TV on or something, and nothing’s really bothering me right then, but I don’t know if I’d call it anxious exactly.

I guess I haven’t noticed it in that exact way.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.0, F41.1, F41.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess what finally got me to come in is I keep jumping out of my skin over stupid stuff. Like at work, if a breaker snaps or somebody drops a tool, or even if someone just walks up behind me, my whole body reacts before I even know what happened. My heart starts pounding and it just keeps going, long after everybody else is already back to normal.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly. I do maintenance work, so loud noises aren’t exactly rare, you know? I used to be better with it. Now I’m the guy flinching like something serious just happened. And then I’m standing there trying to act like I’m fine, but I’m not.\n\nDoctor: Are these reactions happening in response to specific noises or surprises, or are you feeling on edge and jittery even when nothing out of the ordinary is happening?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the specific stuff, yeah… like a breaker snapping, a tool hitting the floor, somebody coming up behind me when I don’t hear them. But then once it happens, I don’t just settle down right away. My chest keeps going like I ran up stairs or something.\n\nI mean, maintenance work is full of that kind of noise, which is the stupid part. There’s always something clanging around, guys yelling across the room, carts banging into doorframes… I used to just tune it out. Now one sharp sound and I’m halfway turned around before I even know what I’m doing, and everyone else is already back to work while I’m still trying to get my heart to calm down.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel on edge or jittery even when nothing out of the ordinary is happening and there are no loud noises around you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I haven’t really sat and measured it like that. Most of the times I notice it, there’s something that kicks it off, like a bang or someone coming up on me.\n\nWhen it’s quiet, I guess I’m not thinking, “Oh, I’m jittery,” you know? I’m usually trying to get through whatever job’s in front of me. Half the time at work there’s always some noise anyway, so it’s hard to tell what counts as nothing happening. Like the other day I was changing out a fixture and the hallway was quiet, but then the cart wheels started squeaking from way down the hall and I still looked over like… anyway, I don’t know if that answers it.\n\nI guess I haven’t noticed it much when there’s really nothing going on.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or on edge when you're alone and there's complete silence?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I’m not alone in complete silence that much, or I don’t notice it that way. There’s always the fridge kicking on, traffic outside, pipes making noise… old apartments make all kinds of sounds, you know? Mine’s got this heater that knocks like somebody’s messing with it, and I’ve been meaning to call the landlord, but then you know how that goes.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I feel anxious just from silence itself. I guess I haven’t paid attention to it like that. Most of what I notice is when something happens suddenly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're in a quiet, peaceful environment with no sudden noises?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it anxious, exactly. Like if it’s actually peaceful, I’m usually… I don’t know, I’m waiting for the next thing I need to do, I guess. I’m not great at just sitting there.\n\nBut quiet is funny, too, because quiet isn’t really quiet. There’s always something—like the vents, somebody outside with a truck backing up, the building settling. At work, even when it’s supposed to be calm, there’s always some little noise from somewhere. So I’m not sure I’ve noticed feeling anxious just because it’s peaceful. It’s more like I notice it when something cuts into that quiet, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you are in a completely quiet and peaceful environment with no distractions or noises?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t really know. Completely quiet like that doesn’t happen much for me. Even when it’s supposed to be quiet, I start hearing little stuff, like the refrigerator or somebody outside or the lights buzzing, and then I’m thinking about whether I left something in the truck or if I need to pick up parts after work.\n\nSo I can’t really say, “Yeah, I’m anxious in total silence,” because I don’t know if I’ve been in that exact kind of situation. I don’t usually sit still long enough to notice it that cleanly, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious if you were in a completely quiet room with no noise at all?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’m trying to picture that, but I don’t know. A completely quiet room sounds almost like one of those test rooms or something, you know, with the padded walls—my buddy used to work doing electrical in a studio, and he said the quiet in there was weird, like you could hear yourself swallowing.\n\nSo maybe I’d feel… uncomfortable? I’m not sure I’d call it anxious. I don’t think I’ve really been somewhere with no noise at all. Even when I’m home and it seems quiet, there’s always something humming or clicking. So I don’t know how I’d react until I was actually sitting in it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're not expecting any sudden noises or surprises?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, if I’m really not expecting anything, that’s kind of when it gets me worse. Like at work, I’ll be doing something normal—checking a panel or reaching for a tool—and if a breaker snaps or somebody drops something behind me, I just about come out of my boots.\n\nAnd it’s embarrassing, because everybody else just looks over and goes back to what they’re doing, and I’m still standing there with my heart going like crazy. I just jump like… like my whole body reacts before I know what happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when everything is going smoothly and you're not expecting any problems?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s kind of the thing… it can be going fine, totally normal, and then something small happens and I’m gone. That’s really what brought me in, honestly. I can’t stop doing that, even when nothing’s actually wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious when nothing is happening and you're not thinking about work or any other stressors?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… when nothing’s happening, I guess I don’t really pay attention to it that way. I don’t usually sit still long enough to notice it that cleanly, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious if you were in a completely quiet room with no noise at all?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’m trying to picture that, but I don’t know. A completely quiet room sounds almost like one of those test rooms or something, you know, with the padded walls—my buddy used to work doing electrical in a studio, and he said the quiet in there was weird, like you could hear yourself swallowing.\n\nSo maybe I’d feel… uncomfortable? I’m not sure I’d call it anxious. I don’t think I’ve really been somewhere with no noise at all. Even when I’m home and it seems quiet, there’s always something humming or clicking. So I don’t know how I’d react until I was actually sitting in it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're not expecting any sudden noises or surprises?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, if I’m really not expecting anything, that’s kind of when it gets me worse. Like at work, I’ll be doing something normal—checking a panel or reaching for a tool—and if a breaker snaps or somebody drops something behind me, I just about come out of my boots.\n\nAnd it’s embarrassing, because everybody else just looks over and goes back to what they’re doing, and I’m still standing there with my heart going like crazy. Same if somebody walks up behind me. They don’t even have to mean anything by it. I just jump like… like my whole body reacts before I know what happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when everything is going smoothly and you're not expecting any problems?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s kind of the thing… it can be going fine, totally normal, and then something small happens and I’m gone. Like a breaker snaps, or a tool hits the floor, or somebody just comes up behind me to ask a question—I jump like I’m about to get hit or something.\n\nAnd then everyone else is already back to work, you know, like no big deal, and I’m standing there trying to act normal with my heart still pounding. That’s really what brought me in, honestly. I can’t stop doing that, even when nothing’s actually wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious when nothing is happening and you're not thinking about work or any other stressors?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… when nothing’s happening, I guess I don’t really pay attention to it that way. Like if I’m just sitting there and not thinking about work, sometimes I’m just watching TV or messing with my phone, and then I’ll realize I didn’t even hear half of what was on.\n\nBut anxious? I’m not sure. My wife used to say I can’t just sit still, but she says that about everything—like if I leave a cabinet open, suddenly it’s a whole thing about how I never finish what I start. So… I don’t know if that’s anxiety or just me being restless. I haven’t really noticed it as “nothing is happening and I’m anxious,” if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're doing something relaxing and there's nothing bothering you?\n\nPatient: I mean… relaxing is kind of a funny word, I guess. Like, I can sit down with the TV on or something, and nothing’s really bothering me right then, but I don’t know if I’d call it anxious exactly.\n\nSometimes I’m just… not really in it. Like my wife will ask, “Did you hear what they just said?” and I didn’t, even though I’m looking right at the screen. But that might just be me being tired or distracted. Now one sharp sound and I’m halfway turned around before I even know what I’m doing, and everyone else is already back to work while I’m still trying to get my heart to calm down.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel on edge or jittery even when nothing out of the ordinary is happening and there are no loud noises around you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I haven’t really sat and measured it like that. Most of the times I notice it, there’s something that kicks it off, like a bang or someone coming up on me.\n\nWhen it’s quiet, I guess I’m not thinking, “Oh, I’m jittery,” you know? I’m usually trying to get through whatever job’s in front of me. Half the time at work there’s always some noise anyway, so it’s hard to tell what counts as nothing happening. Like the other day I was changing out a fixture and the hallway was quiet, but then the cart wheels started squeaking from way down the hall and I still looked over like… anyway, I don’t know if that answers it.\n\nI guess I haven’t noticed it much when there’s really nothing going on.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or on edge when you're alone and there's complete silence?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I’m not alone in complete silence that much, or I don’t notice it that way. There’s always the fridge kicking on, traffic outside, pipes making noise… old apartments make all kinds of sounds, you know? Mine’s got this heater that knocks like somebody’s messing with it, and I’ve been meaning to call the landlord, but then you know how that goes.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I feel anxious just from silence itself. I guess I haven’t paid attention to it like that. Most of what I notice is when something happens suddenly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're in a quiet, peaceful environment with no sudden noises?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it anxious, exactly. Like if it’s actually peaceful, I’m usually… I don’t know, I’m waiting for the next thing I need to do, I guess. I’m not great at just sitting there.\n\nBut quiet is funny, too, because quiet isn’t really quiet. There’s always something—like the vents, somebody outside with a truck backing up, the building settling. At work, even when it’s supposed to be calm, there’s always some little noise from somewhere. So I’m not sure I’ve noticed feeling anxious just because it’s peaceful. It’s more like I notice it when something cuts into that quiet, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you are in a completely quiet and peaceful environment with no distractions or noises?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t really know. Completely quiet like that doesn’t happen much for me. Even when it’s supposed to be quiet, I start hearing little stuff, like the refrigerator or somebody outside or the lights buzzing, and then I’m thinking about whether I left something in the truck or if I need to pick up parts after work.\n\nSo I can’t really say, “Yeah, I’m anxious in total silence,” because I don’t know if I’ve been in that exact kind of situation. I don’t usually sit still long enough to notice it that cleanly, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious if you were in a completely quiet room with no noise at all?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’m trying to picture that, but I don’t know. A completely quiet room sounds almost like one of those test rooms or something, you know, with the padded walls—my buddy used to work doing electrical in a studio, and he said the quiet in there was weird, like you could hear yourself swallowing.\n\nSo maybe I’d feel… uncomfortable? I’m not sure I’d call it anxious. I don’t think I’ve really been somewhere with no noise at all. Even when I’m home and it seems quiet, there’s always something humming or clicking. So I don’t know how I’d react until I was actually sitting in it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're not expecting any sudden noises or surprises?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, if I’m really not expecting anything, that’s kind of when it gets me worse. Like at work, I’ll be doing something normal—checking a panel or reaching for a tool—and if a breaker snaps or somebody drops something behind me, I just about come out of my boots.\n\nAnd it’s embarrassing, because everybody else just looks over and goes back to what they’re doing, and I’m still standing there with my heart going like crazy. Same if somebody walks up behind me. They don’t even have to mean anything by it. I just jump like… like my whole body reacts before I know what happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when everything is going smoothly and you're not expecting any problems?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s kind of the thing… it can be going fine, totally normal, and then something small happens and I’m gone. Like a breaker snaps, or a tool hits the floor, or somebody just comes up behind me to ask a question—I jump like I’m about to get hit or something.\n\nAnd then everyone else is already back to work, you know, like no big deal, and I’m standing there trying to act normal with my heart still pounding. That’s really what brought me in, honestly. I can’t stop doing that, even when nothing’s actually wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious when nothing is happening and you're not thinking about work or any other stressors?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… when nothing’s happening, I guess I don’t really pay attention to it that way. Like if I’m just sitting there and not thinking about work, sometimes I’m just watching TV or messing with my phone, and then I’ll realize I didn’t even hear half of what was on.\n\nBut anxious? I’m not sure. My wife used to say I can’t just sit still, but she says that about everything—like if I leave a cabinet open, suddenly it’s a whole thing about how I never finish what I start. So… I don’t know if that’s anxiety or just me being restless. I haven’t really noticed it as “nothing is happening and I’m anxious,” if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're doing something relaxing and there's nothing bothering you?\n\nPatient: I mean… relaxing is kind of a funny word, I guess. Like, I can sit down with the TV on or something, and nothing’s really bothering me right then, but I don’t know if I’d call it anxious exactly.\n\nSometimes I’m just… not really in it. Like my wife will ask, “Did you hear what they just said?” and I didn’t, even though I’m looking right at the screen. But that might just be me being tired or distracted. I don’t really sit there thinking, “I’m anxious right now,” if that’s what you mean.\n\nI guess I haven’t noticed it in that exact way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when there's nothing specific worrying you or bothering you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. Like… if nothing’s bothering me, I don’t usually stop and check, “Am I anxious?” I just sort of keep moving around or I get into some little thing around the house.\n\nSometimes my wife will say I’m pacing or messing with stuff, but then she’ll also say that when I’m just looking for a screwdriver or trying to fix a drawer that sticks, so I don’t know. I’ve always been kind of like that, needing my hands doing something.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really noticed anxiety when there’s no reason for it. I’d have to think about it more.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I don’t really know. I mean, I guess there are times I feel… off, but I don’t always know if that’s anxious or just me being wound up.

Yeah… I don’t know. I’m trying to think of a clean example, but it’s hard because if I’m feeling that way, I usually end up finding something to pin it on after the fact, you know? Like the truck needs work, or the fence latch is loose, or whatever.

Most of the times I notice it, there’s something that kicks it off, like a bang or someone coming up on me.\n\nWhen it’s quiet, I guess I’m not thinking, “Oh, I’m jittery,” you know? I’m usually trying to get through whatever job’s in front of me. Half the time at work there’s always some noise anyway, so it’s hard to tell what counts as nothing happening. Like the other day I was changing out a fixture and the hallway was quiet, but then the cart wheels started squeaking from way down the hall and I still looked over like… anyway, I don’t know if that answers it.\n\nI guess I haven’t noticed it much when there’s really nothing going on.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or on edge when you're alone and there's complete silence?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I’m not alone in complete silence that much, or I don’t notice it that way. There’s always the fridge kicking on, traffic outside, pipes making noise… old apartments make all kinds of sounds, you know? Mine’s got this heater that knocks like somebody’s messing with it, and I’ve been meaning to call the landlord, but then you know how that goes.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I feel anxious just from silence itself. I guess I haven’t paid attention to it like that. Most of what I notice is when something happens suddenly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're in a quiet, peaceful environment with no sudden noises?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it anxious, exactly. Like if it’s actually peaceful, I’m usually… I don’t know, I’m waiting for the next thing I need to do, I guess. I’m not great at just sitting there.\n\nBut quiet is funny, too, because quiet isn’t really quiet. There’s always something—like the vents, somebody outside with a truck backing up, the building settling. At work, even when it’s supposed to be calm, there’s always some little noise from somewhere. So I’m not sure I’ve noticed feeling anxious just because it’s peaceful. It’s more like I notice it when something cuts into that quiet, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you are in a completely quiet and peaceful environment with no distractions or noises?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t really know. Completely quiet like that doesn’t happen much for me. Even when it’s supposed to be quiet, I start hearing little stuff, like the refrigerator or somebody outside or the lights buzzing, and then I’m thinking about whether I left something in the truck or if I need to pick up parts after work.\n\nSo I can’t really say, “Yeah, I’m anxious in total silence,” because I don’t know if I’ve been in that exact kind of situation. I don’t usually sit still long enough to notice it that cleanly, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious if you were in a completely quiet room with no noise at all?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’m trying to picture that, but I don’t know. A completely quiet room sounds almost like one of those test rooms or something, you know, with the padded walls—my buddy used to work doing electrical in a studio, and he said the quiet in there was weird, like you could hear yourself swallowing.\n\nSo maybe I’d feel… uncomfortable? I’m not sure I’d call it anxious. I don’t think I’ve really been somewhere with no noise at all. Even when I’m home and it seems quiet, there’s always something humming or clicking. So I don’t know how I’d react until I was actually sitting in it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're not expecting any sudden noises or surprises?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, if I’m really not expecting anything, that’s kind of when it gets me worse. Like at work, I’ll be doing something normal—checking a panel or reaching for a tool—and if a breaker snaps or somebody drops something behind me, I just about come out of my boots.\n\nAnd it’s embarrassing, because everybody else just looks over and goes back to what they’re doing, and I’m still standing there with my heart going like crazy. Same if somebody walks up behind me. They don’t even have to mean anything by it. I just jump like… like my whole body reacts before I know what happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when everything is going smoothly and you're not expecting any problems?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s kind of the thing… it can be going fine, totally normal, and then something small happens and I’m gone. Like a breaker snaps, or a tool hits the floor, or somebody just comes up behind me to ask a question—I jump like I’m about to get hit or something.\n\nAnd then everyone else is already back to work, you know, like no big deal, and I’m standing there trying to act normal with my heart still pounding. That’s really what brought me in, honestly. I can’t stop doing that, even when nothing’s actually wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious when nothing is happening and you're not thinking about work or any other stressors?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… when nothing’s happening, I guess I don’t really pay attention to it that way. Like if I’m just sitting there and not thinking about work, sometimes I’m just watching TV or messing with my phone, and then I’ll realize I didn’t even hear half of what was on.\n\nBut anxious? I’m not sure. My wife used to say I can’t just sit still, but she says that about everything—like if I leave a cabinet open, suddenly it’s a whole thing about how I never finish what I start. So… I don’t know if that’s anxiety or just me being restless. I haven’t really noticed it as “nothing is happening and I’m anxious,” if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're doing something relaxing and there's nothing bothering you?\n\nPatient: I mean… relaxing is kind of a funny word, I guess. Like, I can sit down with the TV on or something, and nothing’s really bothering me right then, but I don’t know if I’d call it anxious exactly.\n\nSometimes I’m just… not really in it. Like my wife will ask, “Did you hear what they just said?” and I didn’t, even though I’m looking right at the screen. But that might just be me being tired or distracted. I don’t really sit there thinking, “I’m anxious right now,” if that’s what you mean.\n\nI guess I haven’t noticed it in that exact way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when there's nothing specific worrying you or bothering you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. Like… if nothing’s bothering me, I don’t usually stop and check, “Am I anxious?” I just sort of keep moving around or I get into some little thing around the house.\n\nSometimes my wife will say I’m pacing or messing with stuff, but then she’ll also say that when I’m just looking for a screwdriver or trying to fix a drawer that sticks, so I don’t know. I’ve always been kind of like that, needing my hands doing something.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really noticed anxiety when there’s no reason for it. I’d have to think about it more.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious without anything in particular causing you to feel that way?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I guess there are times I feel… off, but I don’t always know if that’s anxious or just me being wound up.\n\nLike, I’ll be sitting there and then I’ll get up to check something in the garage, and then I’m looking at the water heater or moving tools around, and next thing I know I forgot I was supposed to be watching a show. But that might just be me not liking to sit still. My dad was kind of like that too, always had to have some little project going.\n\nSo without anything causing it? I don’t know. I haven’t really caught myself thinking, “why am I anxious right now?” It’s more like I notice later I couldn’t settle down, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious without anything in particular happening or any specific thought bothering you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I don’t know. I’m trying to think of a clean example, but it’s hard because if I’m feeling that way, I usually end up finding something to pin it on after the fact, you know? Like the truck needs work, or the fence latch is loose, or whatever.\n\nBut if you mean just sitting there and suddenly feeling anxious for no reason… I haven’t really noticed it that clearly. It’s more like I get restless and go do something, and then I’m halfway through sorting a toolbox I already sorted last week. My wife gets on me about that sometimes—says I’m making a bigger mess than I’m fixing.\n\nSo… I guess I’m not sure. I don’t really have a specific thought bothering me every time, but I also don’t always call it anxiety in the moment.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I mean, I guess there are times I feel… off, but I don’t always know if that’s anxious or just me being wound up.\n\nLike, I’ll be sitting there and then I’ll get up to check something in the garage, and then I’m looking at the water heater or moving tools around, and next thing I know I forgot I was supposed to be watching a show. But that might just be me not liking to sit still. My dad was kind of like that too, always had to have some little project going.\n\nSo without anything causing it? I don’t know. I haven’t really caught myself thinking, “why am I anxious right now?” It’s more like I notice later I couldn’t settle down, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious without anything in particular happening or any specific thought bothering you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I don’t know. I’m trying to think of a clean example, but it’s hard because if I’m feeling that way, I usually end up finding something to pin it on after the fact, you know? Like the truck needs work, or the fence latch is loose, or whatever.\n\nBut if you mean just sitting there and suddenly feeling anxious for no reason… I haven’t really noticed it that clearly. It’s more like I get restless and go do something, and then I’m halfway through sorting a toolbox I already sorted last week. My wife gets on me about that sometimes—says I’m making a bigger mess than I’m fixing.\n\nSo… I guess I’m not sure. I don’t really have a specific thought bothering me every time, but I also don’t always call it anxiety in the moment.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you ever suddenly feel anxious without anything happening or any specific thought bothering you?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 39 / installation_maintenance_or_repair_worker / 9th_12th_no_diploma\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean… suddenly, like out of nowhere? I don’t know if I can say that for sure.

Sometimes I’ll be fine, or I think I’m fine, and then I’m up messing with something—like checking if the back door is sticking again or looking for a part I probably don’t even need. But I don’t always have a thought in my head like, “this is what’s bothering me.” It’s more like my body already decided to get moving before I’ve figured out why.

But then again, I’ve always been like that some. Even when I worked with my uncle years ago, he used to say I couldn’t leave anything alone if it was crooked or making a noise. So I don’t know if that’s anxiety exactly. I guess I haven’t noticed it as a sudden thing with no reason.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess what finally got me to come in is I keep jumping out of my skin over stupid stuff. Like at work, if a breaker snaps or somebody drops a tool, or even if someone just walks up behind me, my whole body reacts before I even know what happened. My heart starts pounding and it just keeps going, long after everybody else is already back to normal.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly. I do maintenance work, so loud noises aren’t exactly rare, you know? I used to be better with it. Now I’m the guy flinching like something serious just happened. And then I’m standing there trying to act like I’m fine, but I’m not.\n\nDoctor: Are these reactions happening in response to specific noises or surprises, or are you feeling on edge and jittery even when nothing out of the ordinary is happening?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the specific stuff, yeah… like a breaker snapping, a tool hitting the floor, somebody coming up behind me when I don’t hear them. But then once it happens, I don’t just settle down right away. My chest keeps going like I ran up stairs or something.\n\nI mean, maintenance work is full of that kind of noise, which is the stupid part. There’s always something clanging around, guys yelling across the room, carts banging into doorframes… I used to just tune it out. Now one sharp sound and I’m halfway turned around before I even know what I’m doing, and everyone else is already back to work while I’m still trying to get my heart to calm down.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel on edge or jittery even when nothing out of the ordinary is happening and there are no loud noises around you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I haven’t really sat and measured it like that. Most of the times I notice it, there’s something that kicks it off, like a bang or someone coming up on me.\n\nWhen it’s quiet, I guess I’m not thinking, “Oh, I’m jittery,” you know? I’m usually trying to get through whatever job’s in front of me. Half the time at work there’s always some noise anyway, so it’s hard to tell what counts as nothing happening. Like the other day I was changing out a fixture and the hallway was quiet, but then the cart wheels started squeaking from way down the hall and I still looked over like… anyway, I don’t know if that answers it.\n\nI guess I haven’t noticed it much when there’s really nothing going on.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or on edge when you're alone and there's complete silence?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I’m not alone in complete silence that much, or I don’t notice it that way. There’s always the fridge kicking on, traffic outside, pipes making noise… old apartments make all kinds of sounds, you know? Mine’s got this heater that knocks like somebody’s messing with it, and I’ve been meaning to call the landlord, but then you know how that goes.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I feel anxious just from silence itself. I guess I haven’t paid attention to it like that. Most of what I notice is when something happens suddenly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're in a quiet, peaceful environment with no sudden noises?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it anxious, exactly. Like if it’s actually peaceful, I’m usually… I don’t know, I’m waiting for the next thing I need to do, I guess. I’m not great at just sitting there.\n\nBut quiet is funny, too, because quiet isn’t really quiet. There’s always something—like the vents, somebody outside with a truck backing up, the building settling. At work, even when it’s supposed to be calm, there’s always some little noise from somewhere. So I’m not sure I’ve noticed feeling anxious just because it’s peaceful. It’s more like I notice it when something cuts into that quiet, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you are in a completely quiet and peaceful environment with no distractions or noises?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t really know. Completely quiet like that doesn’t happen much for me. Even when it’s supposed to be quiet, I start hearing little stuff, like the refrigerator or somebody outside or the lights buzzing, and then I’m thinking about whether I left something in the truck or if I need to pick up parts after work.\n\nSo I can’t really say, “Yeah, I’m anxious in total silence,” because I don’t know if I’ve been in that exact kind of situation. I don’t usually sit still long enough to notice it that cleanly, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious if you were in a completely quiet room with no noise at all?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’m trying to picture that, but I don’t know. A completely quiet room sounds almost like one of those test rooms or something, you know, with the padded walls—my buddy used to work doing electrical in a studio, and he said the quiet in there was weird, like you could hear yourself swallowing.\n\nSo maybe I’d feel… uncomfortable? I’m not sure I’d call it anxious. I don’t think I’ve really been somewhere with no noise at all. Even when I’m home and it seems quiet, there’s always something humming or clicking. So I don’t know how I’d react until I was actually sitting in it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're not expecting any sudden noises or surprises?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, if I’m really not expecting anything, that’s kind of when it gets me worse. Like at work, I’ll be doing something normal—checking a panel or reaching for a tool—and if a breaker snaps or somebody drops something behind me, I just about come out of my boots.\n\nAnd it’s embarrassing, because everybody else just looks over and goes back to what they’re doing, and I’m still standing there with my heart going like crazy. Same if somebody walks up behind me. They don’t even have to mean anything by it. I just jump like… like my whole body reacts before I know what happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when everything is going smoothly and you're not expecting any problems?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s kind of the thing… it can be going fine, totally normal, and then something small happens and I’m gone. That’s really what brought me in, honestly. I can’t stop doing that, even when nothing’s actually wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious when nothing is happening and you're not thinking about work or any other stressors?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… when nothing’s happening, I guess I don’t really pay attention to it that way. Like if I’m just sitting there and not thinking about work, sometimes I’m just watching TV or messing with my phone, and then I’ll realize I didn’t even hear half of what was on.\n\nBut anxious? I’m not sure. My wife used to say I can’t just sit still, but she says that about everything—like if I leave a cabinet open, suddenly it’s a whole thing about how I never finish what I start. So… I don’t know if that’s anxiety or just me being restless. I haven’t really noticed it as “nothing is happening and I’m anxious,” if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're doing something relaxing and there's nothing bothering you?\n\nPatient: I mean… relaxing is kind of a funny word, I guess. Like, I can sit down with the TV on or something, and nothing’s really bothering me right then, but I don’t know if I’d call it anxious exactly.\n\nSometimes I’m just… not really in it. Like my wife will ask, “Did you hear what they just said?” and I didn’t, even though I’m looking right at the screen. But that might just be me being tired or distracted. I don’t really sit there thinking, “I’m anxious right now,” if that’s what you mean.\n\nI guess I haven’t noticed it in that exact way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when there's nothing specific worrying you or bothering you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m still not sure I can separate it out like that. Like, if I get keyed up, I usually end up doing something and then I can point at the thing I’m doing and say, “Well, maybe it was that.” But I don’t know if that was really the trigger or just what I latched onto.

I don’t sit there and think, “Okay, nothing happened and now I’m anxious.” I’m not that… I don’t know, tuned in to it, I guess. Sometimes my wife will ask why I’m pacing around or opening cabinets, and I’ll say I’m looking for something, but half the time I forgot what it was.

So I guess the honest answer is I don’t really know. I haven’t noticed it clearly enough to say yes.

And then I’m standing there trying to act like I’m fine, but I’m not.\n\nDoctor: Are these reactions happening in response to specific noises or surprises, or are you feeling on edge and jittery even when nothing out of the ordinary is happening?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the specific stuff, yeah… like a breaker snapping, a tool hitting the floor, somebody coming up behind me when I don’t hear them. My chest keeps going like I ran up stairs or something.\n\nI mean, maintenance work is full of that kind of noise, which is the stupid part. Now one sharp sound and I’m halfway turned around before I even know what I’m doing, and everyone else is already back to work while I’m still trying to get my heart to calm down.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel on edge or jittery even when nothing out of the ordinary is happening and there are no loud noises around you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I haven’t really sat and measured it like that. Most of the times I notice it, there’s something that kicks it off, like a bang or someone coming up on me.\n\nWhen it’s quiet, I guess I’m not thinking, “Oh, I’m jittery,” you know? I’m usually trying to get through whatever job’s in front of me. Half the time at work there’s always some noise anyway, so it’s hard to tell what counts as nothing happening. Like the other day I was changing out a fixture and the hallway was quiet, but then the cart wheels started squeaking from way down the hall and I still looked over like… anyway, I don’t know if that answers it.\n\nI guess I haven’t noticed it much when there’s really nothing going on.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or on edge when you're alone and there's complete silence?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I’m not alone in complete silence that much, or I don’t notice it that way. There’s always the fridge kicking on, traffic outside, pipes making noise… old apartments make all kinds of sounds, you know? Mine’s got this heater that knocks like somebody’s messing with it, and I’ve been meaning to call the landlord, but then you know how that goes.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I feel anxious just from silence itself. There’s always something—like the vents, somebody outside with a truck backing up, the building settling. At work, even when it’s supposed to be calm, there’s always some little noise from somewhere. So I’m not sure I’ve noticed feeling anxious just because it’s peaceful. It’s more like I notice it when something cuts into that quiet, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you are in a completely quiet and peaceful environment with no distractions or noises?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t really know. Completely quiet like that doesn’t happen much for me. Even when it’s supposed to be quiet, I start hearing little stuff, like the refrigerator or somebody outside or the lights buzzing, and then I’m thinking about whether I left something in the truck or if I need to pick up parts after work.\n\nSo I can’t really say, “Yeah, I’m anxious in total silence,” because I don’t know if I’ve been in that exact kind of situation. I don’t usually sit still long enough to notice it that cleanly, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious if you were in a completely quiet room with no noise at all?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’m trying to picture that, but I don’t know. A completely quiet room sounds almost like one of those test rooms or something, you know, with the padded walls—my buddy used to work doing electrical in a studio, and he said the quiet in there was weird, like you could hear yourself swallowing.\n\nSo maybe I’d feel… uncomfortable? I’m not sure I’d call it anxious. I don’t think I’ve really been somewhere with no noise at all. Even when I’m home and it seems quiet, there’s always something humming or clicking. So I don’t know how I’d react until I was actually sitting in it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're not expecting any sudden noises or surprises?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, if I’m really not expecting anything, that’s kind of when it gets me worse. Like at work, I’ll be doing something normal—checking a panel or reaching for a tool—and if a breaker snaps or somebody drops something behind me, I just about come out of my boots.\n\nAnd it’s embarrassing, because everybody else just looks over and goes back to what they’re doing, and I’m still standing there with my heart going like crazy. Same if somebody walks up behind me. They don’t even have to mean anything by it. I just jump like… like my whole body reacts before I know what happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when everything is going smoothly and you're not expecting any problems?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s kind of the thing… it can be going fine, totally normal, and then something small happens and I’m gone. Like a breaker snaps, or a tool hits the floor, or somebody just comes up behind me to ask a question—I jump like I’m about to get hit or something.\n\nAnd then everyone else is already back to work, you know, like no big deal, and I’m standing there trying to act normal with my heart still pounding. That’s really what brought me in, honestly. I can’t stop doing that, even when nothing’s actually wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious when nothing is happening and you're not thinking about work or any other stressors?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… when nothing’s happening, I guess I don’t really pay attention to it that way. Like if I’m just sitting there and not thinking about work, sometimes I’m just watching TV or messing with my phone, and then I’ll realize I didn’t even hear half of what was on.\n\nBut anxious? I’m not sure. My wife used to say I can’t just sit still, but she says that about everything—like if I leave a cabinet open, suddenly it’s a whole thing about how I never finish what I start. So… I don’t know if that’s anxiety or just me being restless. I haven’t really noticed it as “nothing is happening and I’m anxious,” if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're doing something relaxing and there's nothing bothering you?\n\nPatient: I mean… relaxing is kind of a funny word, I guess. Like, I can sit down with the TV on or something, and nothing’s really bothering me right then, but I don’t know if I’d call it anxious exactly.\n\nSometimes I’m just… not really in it. I don’t really sit there thinking, “I’m anxious right now,” if that’s what you mean.\n\nI guess I haven’t noticed it in that exact way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when there's nothing specific worrying you or bothering you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I’ve always been kind of like that, needing my hands doing something.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really noticed anxiety when there’s no reason for it. I’d have to think about it more.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious without anything in particular causing you to feel that way?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. Most of the times I notice it, there’s something that kicks it off, like a bang or someone coming up on me.\n\nWhen it’s quiet, I guess I’m not thinking, “Oh, I’m jittery,” you know? Like the other day I was changing out a fixture and the hallway was quiet, but then the cart wheels started squeaking from way down the hall and I still looked over like… anyway, I don’t know if that answers it.\n\nI guess I haven’t noticed it much when there’s really nothing going on.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or on edge when you're alone and there's complete silence?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I’m not alone in complete silence that much, or I don’t notice it that way. There’s always the fridge kicking on, traffic outside, pipes making noise… old apartments make all kinds of sounds, you know? Mine’s got this heater that knocks like somebody’s messing with it, and I’ve been meaning to call the landlord, but then you know how that goes.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I feel anxious just from silence itself. I guess I haven’t paid attention to it like that. Most of what I notice is when something happens suddenly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're in a quiet, peaceful environment with no sudden noises?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it anxious, exactly. Like if it’s actually peaceful, I’m usually… I don’t know, I’m waiting for the next thing I need to do, I guess. I’m not great at just sitting there.\n\nBut quiet is funny, too, because quiet isn’t really quiet. There’s always something—like the vents, somebody outside with a truck backing up, the building settling. At work, even when it’s supposed to be calm, there’s always some little noise from somewhere. So I’m not sure I’ve noticed feeling anxious just because it’s peaceful. It’s more like I notice it when something cuts into that quiet, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you are in a completely quiet and peaceful environment with no distractions or noises?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t really know. Completely quiet like that doesn’t happen much for me. Even when it’s supposed to be quiet, I start hearing little stuff, like the refrigerator or somebody outside or the lights buzzing, and then I’m thinking about whether I left something in the truck or if I need to pick up parts after work.\n\nSo I can’t really say, “Yeah, I’m anxious in total silence,” because I don’t know if I’ve been in that exact kind of situation. I don’t usually sit still long enough to notice it that cleanly, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious if you were in a completely quiet room with no noise at all?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’m trying to picture that, but I don’t know. A completely quiet room sounds almost like one of those test rooms or something, you know, with the padded walls—my buddy used to work doing electrical in a studio, and he said the quiet in there was weird, like you could hear yourself swallowing.\n\nSo maybe I’d feel… uncomfortable? I’m not sure I’d call it anxious. I don’t think I’ve really been somewhere with no noise at all. Even when I’m home and it seems quiet, there’s always something humming or clicking. So I don’t know how I’d react until I was actually sitting in it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're not expecting any sudden noises or surprises?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, if I’m really not expecting anything, that’s kind of when it gets me worse. Like at work, I’ll be doing something normal—checking a panel or reaching for a tool—and if a breaker snaps or somebody drops something behind me, I just about come out of my boots.\n\nAnd it’s embarrassing, because everybody else just looks over and goes back to what they’re doing, and I’m still standing there with my heart going like crazy. Same if somebody walks up behind me. They don’t even have to mean anything by it. I just jump like… like my whole body reacts before I know what happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when everything is going smoothly and you're not expecting any problems?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s kind of the thing… it can be going fine, totally normal, and then something small happens and I’m gone. Like a breaker snaps, or a tool hits the floor, or somebody just comes up behind me to ask a question—I jump like I’m about to get hit or something.\n\nAnd then everyone else is already back to work, you know, like no big deal, and I’m standing there trying to act normal with my heart still pounding. That’s really what brought me in, honestly. I can’t stop doing that, even when nothing’s actually wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious when nothing is happening and you're not thinking about work or any other stressors?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… when nothing’s happening, I guess I don’t really pay attention to it that way. Like if I’m just sitting there and not thinking about work, sometimes I’m just watching TV or messing with my phone, and then I’ll realize I didn’t even hear half of what was on.\n\nBut anxious? I’m not sure. My wife used to say I can’t just sit still, but she says that about everything—like if I leave a cabinet open, suddenly it’s a whole thing about how I never finish what I start. So… I don’t know if that’s anxiety or just me being restless. I haven’t really noticed it as “nothing is happening and I’m anxious,” if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're doing something relaxing and there's nothing bothering you?\n\nPatient: I mean… relaxing is kind of a funny word, I guess. Like, I can sit down with the TV on or something, and nothing’s really bothering me right then, but I don’t know if I’d call it anxious exactly.\n\nSometimes I’m just… not really in it. Like my wife will ask, “Did you hear what they just said?” and I didn’t, even though I’m looking right at the screen. But that might just be me being tired or distracted. I don’t really sit there thinking, “I’m anxious right now,” if that’s what you mean.\n\nI guess I haven’t noticed it in that exact way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when there's nothing specific worrying you or bothering you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. Like… if nothing’s bothering me, I don’t usually stop and check, “Am I anxious?” I just sort of keep moving around or I get into some little thing around the house.\n\nSometimes my wife will say I’m pacing or messing with stuff, but then she’ll also say that when I’m just looking for a screwdriver or trying to fix a drawer that sticks, so I don’t know. I’ve always been kind of like that, needing my hands doing something.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really noticed anxiety when there’s no reason for it. I’d have to think about it more.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious without anything in particular causing you to feel that way?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I guess there are times I feel… off, but I don’t always know if that’s anxious or just me being wound up.\n\nLike, I’ll be sitting there and then I’ll get up to check something in the garage, and then I’m looking at the water heater or moving tools around, and next thing I know I forgot I was supposed to be watching a show. But that might just be me not liking to sit still. My dad was kind of like that too, always had to have some little project going.\n\nSo without anything causing it? I don’t know. I haven’t really caught myself thinking, “why am I anxious right now?” It’s more like I notice later I couldn’t settle down, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious without anything in particular happening or any specific thought bothering you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I don’t know. I’m trying to think of a clean example, but it’s hard because if I’m feeling that way, I usually end up finding something to pin it on after the fact, you know? Like the truck needs work, or the fence latch is loose, or whatever.\n\nBut if you mean just sitting there and suddenly feeling anxious for no reason… I haven’t really noticed it that clearly. It’s more like I get restless and go do something, and then I’m halfway through sorting a toolbox I already sorted last week. My wife gets on me about that sometimes—says I’m making a bigger mess than I’m fixing.\n\nSo… I guess I’m not sure. I don’t really have a specific thought bothering me every time, but I also don’t always call it anxiety in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever suddenly feel anxious without anything happening or any specific thought bothering you?\n\nPatient: I mean… suddenly, like out of nowhere? I don’t know if I can say that for sure.\n\nSometimes I’ll be fine, or I think I’m fine, and then I’m up messing with something—like checking if the back door is sticking again or looking for a part I probably don’t even need. But I don’t always have a thought in my head like, “this is what’s bothering me.” It’s more like my body already decided to get moving before I’ve figured out why.\n\nBut then again, I’ve always been like that some. Even when I worked with my uncle years ago, he used to say I couldn’t leave anything alone if it was crooked or making a noise. So I don’t know if that’s anxiety exactly. I guess I haven’t noticed it as a sudden thing with no reason.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious without anything triggering it or any specific thought bothering you?\n\nPatient: I’m still not sure I can separate it out like that. Like, if I get keyed up, I usually end up doing something and then I can point at the thing I’m doing and say, “Well, maybe it was that.” But I don’t know if that was really the trigger or just what I latched onto.\n\nI don’t sit there and think, “Okay, nothing happened and now I’m anxious.” I’m not that… I don’t know, tuned in to it, I guess. Sometimes my wife will ask why I’m pacing around or opening cabinets, and I’ll say I’m looking for something, but half the time I forgot what it was.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is I don’t really know. I haven’t noticed it clearly enough to say yes.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
